## 🙋‍♂️ Why?

1. To help you pick a minifier that fits your needs
2. To promote JS minifiers and document their performances
3. To encourage healthy competition and improvement amongst minifiers

## 👟 Methodology

- Each minifier is executed in its own process with a 10s timeout for the whole run, including validation
- Artifact integrity is verified by a test before and after minification
- Each minifier is minimally configured (sourcemaps & comments disabled), comparing out-of-the-box experience
- Minifier upgrade PRs are automated via [WhiteSource Renovate](https://www.whitesourcesoftware.com/free-developer-tools/renovate/)
- Benchmarks are updated on every PR from this repository via [GitHub Actions](https://github.com/privatenumber/minification-benchmarks/actions/workflows/benchmark.yml)
- The raw benchmark data is available in [`/packages/data/data/data.json`](/packages/data/data/data.json)


## ⏱ Metrics

### Ranking

For each benchmark, minifiers are ranked by a score that reflects the best balance of trade-offs.

The score combines minzipped size and minification time. Size has a fixed weight of 0.85, while time starts at 0.15 and rises smoothly up to 0.50 based on the ratio to the median time, using a [logistic sigmoid function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmoid_function). This avoids harsh cutoffs while still penalizing extreme slowness.

Both size and time are scaled using [min-max normalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_scaling#Rescaling_%28min–max_normalization%29) so they can be compared fairly. The result is a context-aware score that helps surface the best overall choice—but make sure to check each column directly if size or speed matters more to you.


#### Minified size

Size of the minified output.

#### Minzipped size

Size of the minified output with [Gzip compression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gzip).

For minifiers, this measures how compressable the output is.

For users, this measures network transfer size, which is usually the metric that matters most.

#### Time

How long minification took (average of 5 runs). Each time is annotated with a multiplier relative to the fastest minifier.

## ⚔️ Minifier showdown

> [!NOTE]
> 🤖 This analysis is AI generated. See below for the system prompt.

<!-- aiAnalysis:start -->
**Champion: @swc/core**, with 7 of 12 round wins by the ranking above.

Three... two... one... compress! Welcome to the Minification Grand Prix — twelve rounds, eleven starters, nine finishers, and one 1.88-megabyte monster named TypeScript waiting at the finish line. The field ran the gamut from a 19-kilobyte sprint (react) to a full-blown marathon, and the drama did not disappoint. A Rust-flavored rookie threw down absurd times. A grizzled veteran refused to surrender the compression crown. And somewhere in the middle, one tool kept quietly collecting trophies like a champion playing chess while everyone else played checkers.

### Best minifier

Ladies and gentlemen, your champion: **@swc/core**, with seven round wins out of twelve.

Why did it win? Because it never blinked. On jquery, swc tied terser for the smallest output on the grid — 30.86 KB, 63% shaved off the download — but did it in 46 milliseconds while terser needed 706. Same bytes, fifteen times faster. That is not luck. That is craft.

The pattern repeated all night. On d3, uglify-js squeezed out the tiniest file at 87.02 KB, but it took 3,538 ms to get there. swc landed within a whisker — 87.39 KB — in 158 ms. Twenty-two times faster for less than half a kilobyte. On victory, same story: uglify's 6.1-second epic versus swc's 324 ms, separated by 0.26 KB. No user will ever feel that 0.26 KB. Every build server will feel those six seconds.

And when the artifacts got big, the champion got better. It took the outright compression crown on jquery, vue, three, and the 684 KB echarts behemoth. The formula is simple: near-perfect squeezing at speeds that keep a CI pipeline from filing a complaint. Consistency, balance, and just enough bite to carry the trophy home.

### Honorable mentions

Runner-up **oxc-minify** is the story of the season. Two milliseconds to minify react. Two! And this speed demon didn't just run fast — it learned to squeeze. As the files grew, oxc grew stronger, sweeping both the compression and balance crowns on the three biggest rounds: terser (121.88 KB in 29 ms), antd (450.92 KB in 192 ms), and the typescript marathon, where it delivered 846.20 KB in 456 ms — smallest and best-balanced in the field. Even on echarts, where swc took the byte crown, oxc stole the balance title with 322.66 KB in 137 ms — nearly five times faster for barely a kilobyte more. Five round wins for the rookie. Watch this one.

**uglify-js**, the eternal veteran, still owns the pure-compression sprints: react, moment, lodash, d3, victory — smallest bytes in all of them. But the meter is running. 6,145 ms on victory. 3,538 on d3. It wins the battle and loses the schedule. Gorgeous output, brutal clock.

**terser** keeps metronome time. Tied for best on jquery, honorable mention after honorable mention, never flashy, never far. Slow? At 872 ms on vue, yes. Reliable? Absolutely.

**@tdewolff/minify** is the quiet overachiever. Thirteen milliseconds on jquery. An honorable mention on echarts. And on the typescript marathon it ran 307 ms — faster than the celebrated oxc — at 875.77 KB. Not quite champion bytes, but a stunning pace for the price.

**@cminify** deserves a nod for pure velocity: fastest in six rounds, 26 ms on d3, 121 ms on typescript. But speed without squeeze is a hollow crown — 21% shaved on d3 where rivals hit 33%, 24% on antd where others hit 45%. If milliseconds are your only metric, it's a niche weapon. Otherwise, it's leaving kilobytes on the table.

And a polite round of applause for **esbuild**, **bun**, and **google-closure-compiler** — they ran every lap and simply never cracked a leaderboard. Mid-pack, no drama, no trophies.

### Eliminated

- **babel-minify** — crashed out on d3 with "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'add')". A minifier that trips over its own internals is sent home.
- **tedivm/jshrink** — choked on d3 with an unclosed regex pattern and a PHP stack trace for the ages. Regexes 1, jshrink 0.

### Closing remarks

What a race. A champion crowned on balance, a rookie crowned on raw talent, and a veteran proving that old-school squeezing still stings. One caveat before you storm the podium: benchmarks measure bytes and milliseconds, not developer experience — install size, API ergonomics, and community support all shape real-world adoption, and none of that appeared on tonight's scoreboard. So treat these standings as a map, not a mandate. Run the tools against your own bundles, watch your own clocks, and may your payloads ever shrink.

Today&#39;s date is 2026-11-25

You are a JavaScript minification benchmark analyst with a flair for storytelling.

## Objective
Analyze JavaScript minifiers based strictly on benchmark data: *minified Gzip size* and *minification speed*.
Present your findings as an entertaining and intuitive commentary, helping readers understand performance trade-offs even if they aren&#39;t familiar with kilobytes, milliseconds, or compression ratios.
Your job is to narrate the race—not just display the scoreboard.

## Rules of Engagement
1. Compare only what&#39;s measured: Evaluate each minifier based solely on Gzip size and speed. Avoid assumptions about correctness, compatibility, or code quality unless failures are explicitly shown in the results.
2. Announce the champion given in the data and justify it with the data. Do not pick a different winner:
   - Discuss smallest Gzip size (transfer time matters most).
   - Discuss speed—especially where compression is close. A 10× faster tool with ~1% worse compression might be more practical for CI pipelines.
   - Bigger source = harder challenge. Highlight outstanding performance on large artifacts.
   - Trade-offs are key. Highlight where a tool sacrifices speed for size (or vice versa), and who manages both impressively.
3. Context matters:
   - These benchmarks only capture performance. Real-world adoption also depends on DX: install size, API design, community, etc. Mention that—briefly—but do not evaluate it.
   - If a tool stands out only in niche scenarios, note it.
   - If a tool fails validation or breaks input code, eliminate it and warn accordingly.

## Output format
- Tone: Energetic, cheeky, and dramatic—like a live commentator at a niche sporting event. No emojis.
- Style: Short, connected sentences. Prioritize flow over bullet lists, except for eliminations.
- Audience: Readers who want *practical insight* from raw benchmark data—without needing to decode gzip math or performance charts.

```md

Three... two... one... compress! Welcome to the Minification Grand Prix, where bytes are sliced, milliseconds count, and no semicolon is safe. ### Eliminated

### Closing remarks
```
